Bindings introduced type-only are type-level names: a marker-shaped expression statement `SPEC.a` and a call `t(SPEC.a)` rooted at them — in the declaration-modifier arm `t` is bound by an ordinary `import { text as t } from "./NAME.xspec"` beside the type-only default binding (two declarations of one module binding distinct identifiers, valid under 4), so an ordinary `text` callee receives a chain rooted at a type-only binding; in the named-binding arm `SPEC` is itself bound type-only, by a second declaration `import type SPEC from "./NAME.xspec"` beside the `{ type text as t }` one, so callee and argument are both rooted at type-only bindings — record no edge (`query edges` reports none from the file) and trigger no xspec finding — not 14.8 and not 14.18: such value-level uses fall under no condition (4.5, 14.18) — and `build`/`check` exit 0, the workspace staying valid; the consumer-side TypeScript error is outside xspec's validations (as in T6.4-5). Control: the same statements under ordinary bindings record their `references`/`embeds` edges. The mixed combination — an ordinary node as the argument of a type-only `text` binding — is pinned on a workspace of its own, its finding failing that workspace. One sentence of 4.5 makes a binding introduced type-only, like a local declaration shadowing an import binding, no spec module reference — the shadowing case pinned by T4.5-4's callee arm: a call through such a binding is no spec module's `text` call, so the ordinary node it receives is used outside the sanctioned uses (14.18: none of the uses 4.5 sanctions — a dependency marker, a child property access, an argument of a spec module's `text` call, and a `text` binding as that call's callee), while the callee's own value use falls under no condition (4.5, 14.18). With `import SPEC from "./NAME.xspec"` beside `import { type text as t } from "./NAME.xspec"`, and separately beside `import type { text as t } from "./NAME.xspec"`, the call `t(SPEC.a)`: `build` and `check` report exactly one finding, 14.18 located at `SPEC.a` (T14-11), exit 1; no edge and no occurrence — `occurrences --file` on the failing workspace lists none for the call beside the finding (11.2, T5.7-4) — failing a product that takes the call for a sanctioned one, reporting nothing, or records its `embeds` edge.
* **T4-5 Import collisions, type-only or not.** A colliding identifier roots no chain whether or not either import is type-only (2.4, 4.5, 4): the type-only exemption reaches a chain the language roots at one binding, and a colliding identifier roots it at none. In a code-group file holding the marker `SPEC.a` and the call `text(SPEC.b)`, `text` bound by a separate `import { text } from "./A.xspec"` (two declarations of one module binding distinct identifiers, valid under 4), `specs/A.mdx` and `specs/B.mdx` each holding `a` and `b` so that either spec binding alone would resolve both chains, one arm per pairing, each staged in both declaration orders: the value-level pairings — `import SPEC from "./A.xspec"` beside `import SPEC from "./B.xspec"`, and beside a value-level non-spec `import { SPEC } from "./t"` (4.5's plain case, two imports binding one identifier, neither type-only); `import SPEC from "./A.xspec"` beside `import type SPEC from "./B.xspec"`; beside a non-spec `import type { SPEC } from "./t"`; the spec import itself type-only (`import type SPEC from "./A.xspec"`) beside a value-level non-spec `import { SPEC } from "./t"`; and both type-only (`import type SPEC from "./A.xspec"` beside `import type { SPEC } from "./t"`). Every arm: `build` and `check` report 14.15 locating both declarations and 14.7 for each chain, exit 1; no edge and no occurrence — `occurrences --file` on the failing workspace lists none for the two spellings beside the findings (11.2) — never the edges, or the type-only exemption's silence (T4-4), that a product rooting the chain at whichever binding TypeScript's own resolution prefers, or at the first or last declaration, exhibits instead.
* **T4-6 Nested module-linking forms and spec module imports.** 4 defines a TypeScript file's module-linking forms, and its spec module imports, by construct, wherever each stands — TypeScript's parser admits import, export, and `import X = require(…)` declarations, and string-named module declarations, beyond a file's top-level statement list, the restrictions on where each may stand being post-parse grammar checks 14.20 excludes. Arms in code sources under `src/`, each arm staging a workspace of its own, its files together, `specs/NAME.mdx` discovered and holding `a`, each file text TypeScript 5.9.3 accepts both as module code and as script code (S-9) and none compiled under H-2's channel, TypeScript's checker rejecting each nested form after parsing, so that each arm observes xspec's surfaces alone (T4.5-10): (a) a nested spec module import is held to 4's rules — `import X from "../specs/missing.xspec"` (no such source) and, separately, `import { a } from "../specs/NAME.xspec"` (a named binding other than `text`), each standing in a function body and, in a further file each, in a namespace body: each fails with 14.15 located at the declaration's own characters (T14-11); (b) a nested `text` binding is its module's `text` export (4.3) — `src/t.ts` = `export function g() {`, U+000A, `  import N, { text } from "../specs/NAME.xspec"`, U+000A, `  text(N.a)`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A: `build` and `check` exit 0, `query edges` reports one `embeds` edge from `src/t.ts#g` to `specs/NAME.mdx#a`, and `occurrences` one call occurrence spanning `text(N.a)`, its source `src/t.ts#g` — failing a product that resolves a callee through top-level imports alone; the binding's invisible side, a call outside the import's scope, is T4.5-10(g); (c) every other module-linking form whose specifier ends in `.xspec` is invalid wherever it stands (4) — `import X = require("../specs/NAME.xspec")` and `export * from "../specs/NAME.xspec"`, each inside a `namespace N { }` and, separately, inside a function body, and `declare module "../specs/NAME.xspec" { }` inside a `namespace N { }`, one file each: each fails with 14.15 located by the declaration's own characters (14, T14-11); (d) a module-linking form whose relative specifier designates a derived-file path other than through `.xspec` is invalid wherever it stands (4) — `src/d.ts` = `export function f() { import "../specs/NAME.xspec.ts" }`, U+000A, the side-effect import nested in a function body: it fails with 14.15 located at the declaration's own characters, [22, 53) (T14-11). A product examining a file's top-level statements alone for module-linking forms and spec module imports passes T4-2 and fails every arm here, and one judging derived-path designations over the top-level statements alone fails (d) while passing T4-2 and (a) through (c), whose nested specifiers end in `.xspec`. A file move rewrites a nested declaration's specifier as it does a top-level one (T6.5-1), and a move that would make a nested form's specifier designate a derived-file path is refused as a top-level form's is (T6.5-20(d)).

### 4.1 Node skeleton

* **T4.1-1** The default export is the root; child sections appear as properties named by ID segment; a consumer chain to a leaf type-checks; a chain naming a missing requirement path is a TypeScript type error against the generated module. Segments naming built-in members are child properties like any other (1.4 forbids none of them): top-level sections `toString`, `valueOf`, `hasOwnProperty`, `name`, and `length`, each holding a child `x` — a consumer chain to each child (`SPEC.toString.x`, `SPEC.name.x`, …) type-checks, and `text(SPEC.<segment>)` returns that node's subtree text at runtime (T4.3-1) — failing a product whose node representation lets an inherited or function-own member (`Object.prototype`'s methods; a function's `name` and `length`) stand in for, or refuse, the child.
* **T4.1-2 Readonly.** Assigning to a node property is a type error.
* **T4.1-3 No text as values.** Every node reachable by child property access is an opaque token whose only supported operations are child property access and passing to `text()` (4.1): for each node in a fixture, the value obtained by the supported child-access operation is accepted by `text()`, and the supported operations behave as specified; no requirement text is observable through the module's values — a consumer that never imports `text` and performs only supported operations obtains no node's own or subtree text from the values reachable by those operations. The observation is concrete, so the arm cannot pass as a tautology: over every runtime value reachable from the default export by child property access, the harness asserts that a reflective deep walk of it — property keys and values, own and inherited, its string coercion, and its JSON serialization — encounters no string equal to or containing any node's own or subtree text, requirement text being obtainable at runtime only via the `text` export (4.1, 4.3). The fixture's texts are distinctive, so the walk never fails a conforming product on a string it meets incidentally: every node — the root and every parent included — has a non-empty own text carrying a token of its own that no ID segment, path, or identifier spells (its subtree text, and every ancestor's, carrying the token too), so that no property name, path, inherited method's source text, or JSON serialization's punctuation equals or contains any node's own or subtree text, as every string would contain an empty text and incidental strings a short or whitespace-only one. An opaque token may be prototype-less, circular, guarded by throwing accessors, or a primitive — a string token carrying no requirement text included — 4.1 fixing no runtime representation and supporting no operation beyond the two, so none of those bars a conforming node — and a walk operation that throws on a value (string coercion of a prototype-less object, JSON serialization of a circular one, a property read through a throwing getter) contributes nothing to the walk: it never fails the test and never errors the harness (H-11), the walk continuing over every other operation and value.

### 4.2 Documentation and navigation

* **T4.2-1 Doc comments.** The generated module carries, for every node, a documentation comment containing the node's own text (fixture with expanded embedding to confirm own text is the expanded value, 1.6).
* **T4.2-2 Truncation.** A node whose own text exceeds 1000 Unicode code points (containing multi-code-unit characters, e.g. emoji, to distinguish code points from UTF-16 units and bytes) is truncated to exactly its first 1000 code points with `…` appended; a node at exactly 1000 code points is not truncated and gains no `…`.
* **T4.2-3 Escaping.** Own text containing `*/` appears in the comment with each occurrence written `*\/`, and the generated module still parses under standard tooling. Combined with truncation (4.2: the own text is truncated to its first 1000 code points, and each `*/` in the emitted text is written `*\/`), each read as above: a node of exactly 1000 code points holding `*/` carries all 1000 code points, the `*/` written `*\/`, and no `…`; and a longer node holding `*/` before its 1000th code point carries its first 1000 code points, the `*/` written `*\/`, then `…`. A product escaping before it truncates fails both: it cuts the first node's last code point and appends `…`, and ends the second's text one code point early.
* **T4.2-4 Navigation.** Under standard TypeScript language tooling, go-to-definition on a consumer's node reference resolves into the source `.mdx` file: for a non-root reference, at the corresponding `<S>` section; for a root-node reference (the default export), at the start of the file (4.2); hover on the reference shows the documentation (asserted via the tooling's definition/hover queries).

### 4.3 text

* **T4.3-1** `text(node)` returns the node's subtree text as a `string` at runtime (byte-compared to expected expansion) and records an `embeds` edge from the calling code location to the node (`query edges`).
* **T4.3-2** A string argument to `text` in a TypeScript file fails with 14.8; so does a dynamic node-form argument there — a computed index by variable, a computed index by numeric literal (`text(SPEC[1])`, `SPEC`'s module holding a top-level section `1`, a call TypeScript type-checks as `text(SPEC["1"])`), a computed index by template literal (`` text(SPEC[`a`]) ``, `SPEC`'s module holding `a` — 2.4: template literals are not static, so a product reading a no-substitution template literal as a static string literal records the edge), an optional-chaining chain, a parenthesized chain (`text((SPEC.a))` and the partly parenthesized `text((SPEC).a)`; 2.4: parentheses applied to the chain make the reference dynamic, though TypeScript's checker types each argument as `SPEC.a`), and a parenthesized index, `text(SPEC[("a")])` (2.4: no static string literal indexes it, though the checker types the argument as `SPEC.a`), never 14.18 (4.5: a non-static `text(...)` argument is an invalid argument, not unsupported usage) — and the TypeScript-only forms 2.4 makes dynamic in a TypeScript source, never a parse failure there: `text(SPEC.a!)`, `text(SPEC.a as X)`, `text(<X>SPEC.a)` (in a `.ts` file, where the angle-bracket assertion parses), and `text(SPEC.a satisfies X)` — each 14.8 located at the call (T14-11), no edge, no occurrence, the file well-formed (the spec-source counterparts are 14.20, T2.4-2); and so do a zero-argument and a two-argument `text(...)` call in a TypeScript file (14.8's arity clause holds in either language, 2.4/4.5 — the MDX arms are T2.4-3). Every argument of a spec module's `text` call is a sanctioned use (4.5, 14.18): for `text(SPEC.a, SPEC.b)`, both nodes of the callee's own module, `build` and `check` report exactly one finding, 14.8 located at the call (T14-11), no 14.18 beside it; and for its cross-module variant `text(SPEC.a, B.b)`, `B` another spec module's default binding, the same single finding, neither 14.18 nor 14.11 (14.11: a call condition 8 reports for its argument count is that condition alone) — failing a product that sanctions a sole argument alone and reports 14.18 at the second.

### 4.4 Module branding

* **T4.4-1** Passing a node from module A to module B's `text` export: TypeScript type error (14.11); when compiled with the error suppressed at the consumer's responsibility (or executed via the emitted JS), the call throws at runtime with an error whose message contains both modules' source files' workspace-relative paths, `/`-separated (4.4, 1.5) — `specs/A.mdx` (the node's module) and `specs/B.mdx` (the called module) as substrings — a product naming the generated module files, using native separators, or naming one module alone failing the assertion. Finding contract (14.11, 5.7): `build` and `check` report the condition-11 finding located at the call, callee through closing parenthesis (T14-11), exit 1, its `identities` exactly `["specs/B.mdx"]` — one element, the root identity of the called module, never the node's module and never a generated-module path (T12.7-1); the call's `embeds` edge and occurrence stand beside the finding — the workspace failing `build`, observed through `occurrences` (11.2): exactly one record for the call, `kind` `"embeds"`, `source` the calling unit's identity and range, `target` the node's identity (`specs/A.mdx#a`), the finding accompanying, exit 1. A resolving argument is required: `textB(A.missing)` reports condition 7 alone and `textB(A.a!)` condition 8 alone, no condition 11 beside either and no occurrence for either. Invalid called path (Linux leg, T11.2-3): with `textB` imported from a discovered `specs/B#.mdx` (the import valid — the file is discovered — its path condition 19), `textB(A.a)` reports condition 11 with `identities` exactly `[]`, the finding still reported and the occurrence still recorded (no identity over an invalid path is ever emitted, 1.5, 11.2). A call through a colliding `text` identifier is never this condition (T4.5-9).
* **T4.4-2** Consuming two spec modules in one file with aliased `text` imports: each alias accepts only its own module's nodes.

### 4.5 Dependency markers

* **T4.5-1 Marker semantics.** A bare requirement reference as an expression statement records a `references` edge from the enclosing code location; at runtime the program behaves as if the line were absent (harmless property read) with no additional tooling installed.
* **T4.5-2 Root marker.** A bare reference to the default export records a `references` edge to the root node only (4.5): with the marker the only reference in its code location, `query edges --from` that location lists exactly one edge, `references` to the file's bare-path root identity, failing a product that also records edges to the file's sections; it grants no coverage in any profile (roots never appear in coverage paths and root-targeted edges never extend one, 8; T8-5), but the code location is impacted (9.2) by any text edit in the document — an edit changing the root's subtreeHash or effectiveHash (4.5). Upstream arm (4.5: in the document or upstream of it): with the marker's document bearing a root-sourced dependency edge into another file (a top-level `{text(...)}`, as T8-5), an edit in that file changing only the root's effectiveHash leaves the location impacted — transitively (9.2), no node of the marker's document `changed`.
* **T4.5-3 Static rule in TS.** A non-static bare reference in expression-statement position fails with 14.8 (invalid argument, not 14.18), located as the statement's expression exclusive of its terminator (T14-11), no edge and no occurrence: a computed index by variable; a computed index by numeric literal, `SPEC[1];`, `SPEC`'s module holding a top-level section `1` (2.4: an index form other than a static string literal — TypeScript's checker reads it as `SPEC["1"]`, so the consumer type-checks clean and a product resolving through the checker records the edge and reports nothing); a computed index by template literal, `` SPEC[`login-v2`]; ``, `SPEC`'s module holding `login-v2` (2.4: template literals are not static, so a product reading a no-substitution template literal as a static string literal records the edge and reports nothing); optional chaining (`SPEC?.a;`); parentheses applied to the chain (2.4) — the whole chain parenthesized, `(SPEC.a);`, and a partly parenthesized one, `(SPEC).a;` — located as `(SPEC.a)` and `(SPEC).a`, the parentheses included, failing a product that looks through parentheses and records the edge or classes the parenthesized statement as an unsanctioned use (the spec-source counterparts: T2.4-2's parenthesized chain, T14-11's `d={(BASE.a)}`); a parenthesized index, `SPEC[("a")];`, `SPEC`'s module holding `a` — an index form other than a static string literal (2.4), though TypeScript's checker reads it as `SPEC["a"]`, so a product unwrapping the parentheses records the edge and reports nothing (the spec-source counterpart: T2.4-2's parenthesized index); and the TypeScript-only forms 2.4 makes dynamic in a TypeScript source — a non-null assertion `SPEC.a!;`, a type assertion `SPEC.a as X;`, an angle-bracket assertion `<X>SPEC.a;` (in a `.ts` file), and `SPEC.a satisfies X;` — each a well-formed file, never 14.20 (the spec-source counterparts, T2.4-2).
* **T4.5-4 Shadowing.** A local declaration shadowing the import binding: chains rooted at the local are not spec references — no edge, no error, program builds; among the locals, a `using SPEC = f()` in a block and an `await using SPEC = f()` in an async function (2.4, 4.5: variable declarations, `using` and `await using` included; each file accepted by TypeScript 5.9.3 both as module code and as script code (14.20)), each shadowing the import in its scope: a chain rooted there records no edge and raises no finding, while the same chain outside that scope records its edge. Callee side (4.5: rooting is scope-aware and value-level for the `text` binding as for the node chain): with an inner-scope `function text(x: unknown) {}` shadowing the imported `text`, a call `text(SPEC.a)` in that scope has a non-spec callee and a node argument — the "passing to any other function" of 4.5, 14.18 (T4.5-5): `build` and `check` report the condition-18 finding located at that use, exit 1; the call records no `embeds` edge and no occurrence (5.7) — `occurrences --file` on that file, answering on the failing workspace (11.2), lists none for it and carries the finding — and the shadowed `text` import binding, never used, stays a valid import (2.1, 4). Control, in the same file: the identical call outside the shadowing scope is an ordinary `text` call, its `embeds` occurrence listed. A product resolving the callee by name records an `embeds` edge and no finding, failing the arm; a type-only callee, which the same sentence of 4.5 makes no spec module reference either, meets the same outcome (T4-4's mixed combination).
* **T4.5-5 Sanctioned uses only.** Each fails with 14.18: aliasing a node to a variable; destructuring the module; re-exporting the binding; storing a node in an array/object; passing a node to a function other than a spec module's `text` export; using `text` as a value (passing/storing it) other than as a callee. Every argument of a spec module's `text` call is sanctioned, a second one included (4.5: an argument of a call whose callee is a spec module's `text` export; T4.3-2's two-argument arms).
* **T4.5-6 text in statement position.** A `text(...)` call as an expression statement is valid, records an `embeds` edge, and is not a marker (kind asserted via `query edges --kinds`).
* **T4.5-7 Type-level freedom.** `typeof SPEC.a.b` and other type-level references to a spec module import's bindings build with no edges recorded and are not rewritten by rename (see T6.4-5); an import type naming a `.xspec` module is no such reference but a module-linking form, 14.15 (4.5, T4-2).
* **T4.5-8 Same-scope collisions.** An identifier a spec module import binds that a non-import declaration of the same scope — the import's own (4.5): the module scope for every import staged here, each standing at top level; a nested import's, a function's parameters included, is T4.5-11's — also binds at value level roots no resolving chain (2.4, 4.5, 14.15). In a code source importing `SPEC` from `./A.xspec` with a marker `SPEC.a` and a call `text(SPEC.b)`, one arm per colliding form at module scope — `const SPEC = 1`, `using SPEC = f()` and `await using SPEC = f()` (2.4: variable declarations, `using` and `await using` included; each file accepted by TypeScript 5.9.3 both as module code and as script code (14.20), top-level placement included), `function SPEC() {}`, `class SPEC {}`, `enum SPEC {}`, and `namespace SPEC { export const v = 1 }` (a namespace binding a value): `build` and `check` report the condition-15 collision beside condition 7 for each chain (unresolved), exit 1; no edge and no occurrence for its spellings (5.7, T5.7-4) — observed through `occurrences --file`, which answers on this failing workspace (11.2), listing none for them beside the findings, while `query edges` is gated there (13.3) and observes nothing; and the condition-15 finding locates every colliding declaration — the import by its own characters and the non-import by the construct binding the name (14, T14-11), byte-asserted against precomputed offsets: the variable declarator's own characters (`SPEC = 1`, the `const` statement excluded; `SPEC = f()` for each `using` arm, the `using` or `await using` excluded) and the function, class, enum, or namespace declaration's own characters, an `export` prefix excluded. Further located forms, one arm each (14's declarator, decorator, and modifier rules): a declarator without initializer, `let SPEC;` — located at `SPEC` alone; a binding pattern, `const { SPEC } = o` — at `{ SPEC } = o`; a decorated class, `@dec class SPEC {}` — from its `@`; `export`-prefixed declarations, `export class SPEC {}` and `export function SPEC() {}` — from `class` and `function`, the `export` and what separates it excluded; and declarations carrying a modifier other than a leading `export`, each located from that modifier (14: a declaration by its own characters, its decorators and modifiers included and a leading `export` alone excluded) — `async function SPEC() {}` and `export async function SPEC() {}`, each from `async`; `abstract class SPEC {}`, from `abstract`; `const enum SPEC {}`, from `const` (an enum declaration, const or not, binds at value level, 2.4); and `declare namespace SPEC { export const v: number }`, a namespace binding a value, from `declare` — each file accepted by TypeScript 5.9.3 both as module code and as script code (14.20), each arm reporting as those above, and a product starting a declaration's range at its keyword failing these. Type-level controls in the same file (2.4, 4.5: colliding with nothing): `interface SPEC {}`, `type SPEC = number`, and `namespace SPEC { export type T = number }` (a namespace binding no value) each leave the import rooting the chain — the edges recorded, no finding, exit 0 — while an inner-scope `const SPEC = 1` shadows instead (T4.5-4). The spec-source case: a file holding `export const BASE = 1` beside `import BASE from "./BASE.xspec"` reports 14.16 for the export statement, 14.15 for the collision (locating the import and the declarator), and 14.5/14.6 for the `d` and `text(...)` spellings rooted at `BASE`, no edge and no occurrence recorded for them — the two declarations staged in one ESM block (the export on the line after the import), the staging 14.20 makes a finding in a well-formed file and never a parse failure (T14-12), with a second arm across two blocks reporting identically. Type-only side (2.4, 14.15: the declaration collision needs the import's binding value-level, 4 — unlike the import–import collision, which holds type-only or not, T4-5): the same code source with the spec import type-only — `import type SPEC from "./A.xspec"` — and the call `text(SPEC.b)` left out, the marker-shaped `SPEC.a` its one spelling rooted at `SPEC` (the arm pins the declarations' non-collision, which needs no `text` call), beside each value-level form above at module scope, one arm each; and, for the named-binding modifier, a code source holding `import { type text as t } from "./A.xspec"` and the call `t("x")` beside `function t(x: unknown) {}` and, separately, beside `const t = (x: unknown) => ""`. Every arm collides with nothing: `build` and `check` report no finding, exit 0, the workspace valid — the consumer-side TypeScript diagnostics, a declaration conflict included, lying outside xspec's validations (T4-4); the marker-shaped `SPEC.a`, rooted at no spec module binding at value level (4.5), records no edge (`query edges` lists none from the file) and no occurrence and raises no 14.7 and no 14.8, and `t("x")`, no spec module's `text` call, raises no 14.8 — failing a product that extends the import–import rule's indifference to type-only (T4-5) to declarations, reporting 14.15 on this valid workspace.
* **T4.5-9 Call through a colliding `text` identifier.** A call whose callee identifier a spec module import binds to its `text` export and another import, or a value-level declaration, of the import's own scope also binds is no spec module's `text` call (4.5) — here every import standing at top level, so that scope is the module's (a function's, a parameter included: T4.5-11(e); a `text` binding of another scope, enclosing or nested, leaving each call to its own scope's binding: T4.5-11(h), (i)): it records no edge and no occurrence and falls under no condition of a `text` call, while a spec module binding or node its argument spells is used outside the sanctioned uses (14.18), beside the collision (14.15). In a code-group file importing `SPEC, { text }` from `./A.xspec`, one arm per colliding form at module scope: a second, non-spec import binding `text` (`import { text } from "./t"`); a second spec module's `text` (`import B, { text } from "./B.xspec"`); a type-only import (`import type { text } from "./t"`); and the value-level declarations `function text(x: unknown) {}` and `const text = (x: unknown) => ""`. In each, the call `text(SPEC.a)`: `build` and `check` report 14.15 locating both declarations and 14.18 located at `SPEC.a` (the identifier extended by its longest static chain, T14-11), exit 1 — no 14.6, no 14.7, no 14.8, no 14.11; no edge and no occurrence — `occurrences --file` on the failing workspace lists none for it beside the findings (11.2, T5.7-4). Whatever the argument: `text("x")` beside the same collision reports no 14.8 (and no 14.18, the argument spelling no binding or node); a cross-module argument `text(B.a)`, `B` a valid second module's default binding, reports 14.18 at `B.a`, never 14.11 (T4.4-1). Control in the same workspace: `type text = number` beside the import collides with nothing (2.4) — the call records its `embeds` edge and occurrence, no finding — and the inner-scope shadowing declaration is T4.5-4's.
* **T4.5-10 Nested import declarations: scope and rooting.** An import declaration declares its bindings in its own scope (4.5): the file's for a top-level declaration and, for a nested one — TypeScript's parser admits an import declaration wherever a statement may stand (14.20) — that of the innermost function, class static block, or namespace or module declaration enclosing it, never a block's, as TypeScript 5.9.3 declares them. Arms in code sources under `src/`, each arm staging a workspace of its own, its files together, `specs/A.mdx` holding `a` and its child `a.b`, each file text TypeScript 5.9.3 accepts both as module code and as script code (S-9) — its diagnostic confining import declarations to a file's top level being a post-parse check 14.20 excludes — and none compiled under H-2's channel, that diagnostic rejecting it: each arm observes xspec's surfaces alone, as do those of T4-6, T4.5-11, and T6.5-24 through T6.5-26. (a) SPEC.md's example — `src/g.ts` = `function g() { { import A from "../specs/A.xspec"; } A.a.b; }`, U+000A: the marker after the block is rooted at the nested import, whose scope is `g`'s, not the block's — `build` and `check` exit 0, `query edges` reports one `references` edge from `src/g.ts#g` (4.6) to `specs/A.mdx#a.b`, and `occurrences` one marker occurrence spanning `A.a.b`, its source `src/g.ts#g` — failing a product that ignores nested imports, or scopes one to its block and so finds the marker rooted at nothing. (b) Each container 4.5 names, one file each, the chain following the import within the container: a function's body (`function g() { import A from "../specs/A.xspec"; A.a.b }` → `path#g`), an arrow function's (`const h = () => { { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" } A.a.b }` → `path#h`), a class method's (`class C { m() { { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" } A.a.b } }` → `path#C.m`), a class static block (`class C { static { { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" } A.a.b } }` → `path#C`, T4.6-1), a namespace (`namespace N { { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" } A.a.b }` → `path#N`) and its legacy spelling `module M { import A from "../specs/A.xspec"; A.a.b }` (→ `path#M`, the same declaration as `namespace`, 4.6), and a module declaration — 4.5's term beside the namespace, which the legacy spelling already is: a string-named module declaration (4) — `declare module "m" { import A from "../specs/A.xspec"; A.a.b }` (→ `path`, the declaration binding no unit, 4.6), TypeScript's diagnostics there — on a statement in an ambient context, and on an ambient module's import naming a module by a relative name — post-parse checks as well (14.20, S-9); and, the import's scope never a block's at top level either, an import inside a top-level `if` block and, separately, inside a bare block — `if (c) { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `A.a.b`, U+000A, and `{ import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `A.a.b`, U+000A — binding in the file's scope (4.5: never a block's, as TypeScript at release 5.9.3 declares them — no function, class static block, or namespace or module declaration enclosing it, TypeScript 5.9.3's binder places it in the file's own symbol table), so that the chain after the block is rooted at it (→ `path`); and, an import declaring its bindings throughout its own scope (4.5), never from its position onward, a chain preceding the import within that scope — `function g() { A.a.b; import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }` (→ `path#g`), rooted at the import as TypeScript 5.9.3 resolves it, where a product binding an import only from its position onward finds the chain rooted at nothing and records no edge: on the arm's workspace, holding every file, `build` and `check` exit 0 and each marker records its `references` edge to `specs/A.mdx#a.b` from the location named (`query edges`). (c) The visibility boundary — the chain spelled where the import's scope does not reach, one file each: after the function (`function g() { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `A.a.b`, U+000A), in a sibling function (`function g() { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }` beside `function h() { A.a.b }`), in a method of the class whose static block holds the import (`class C { static { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" } m() { A.a.b } }`), in a second declaration of the namespace whose first holds it (`namespace N { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `namespace N { A.a.b }` — a namespace declaration's scope, never the merged namespace's), and after a string-named module declaration holding it, `declare` or not (4) — `declare module "m" { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `A.a.b`, U+000A, and `module "m" { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `A.a.b`, U+000A, the undeclared form's diagnostic post-parse as well (T4-2) — the import's scope the declaration's, never the file's (4.5: as TypeScript 5.9.3 declares it): no binding of `A` is visible there, so each chain is rooted at nothing — no edge, no occurrence, no finding; `build` and `check` exit 0 and `query edges` lists no edge from its location — failing a product that roots by name file-wide, or counts identifier-named namespace declarations alone among the containers, which records the edges, and one reading the chain as an unresolved spec module reference, which reports 14.7. (d) The cross-scope pair (4.5: the nested import roots its scope's chains whether or not a top-level import binds the identifier too; 14.15: the two imports collide whatever scope each declares it in) — `src/x.ts` = `import A from "../specs/B.xspec"`, U+000A, `function g() {`, U+000A, `  { import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `  A.a.b`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A, `A.a.b`, U+000A, `specs/B.mdx` holding `a` and `a.b` too: `build` and `check` report the condition-15 finding locating both declarations by their own characters (T14-11), exit 1, and no 14.7; `occurrences --file src/x.ts`, answering on the failing workspace (11.2), lists exactly two records beside the finding — the marker inside `g` targeting `specs/A.mdx#a.b`, its source `src/x.ts#g`, and the top-level marker targeting `specs/B.mdx#a.b`, its source `src/x.ts` — failing a product that roots both at the outer import, the inner record then targeting `specs/B.mdx#a.b`, and one rooting both at neither, reading a colliding identifier unresolved file-wide where 2.4 makes it so within one scope alone (T4-5, T4.5-11(c)). (e) The cross-scope pair whose nested import is no spec module import (14.15: either import a spec module import, whatever scope each declares the identifier in) — on a workspace of its own, `src/y.ts` = `import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `export function g() {`, U+000A, `  import { SPEC } from "./t"`, U+000A, `  SPEC.a`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A, `SPEC.a.b`, U+000A: `build` and `check` report the condition-15 finding locating both declarations by their own characters (T14-11), exit 1, and no 14.7; within `g` the identifier resolves to the non-spec binding (4.5: rooting is scope-aware), so `SPEC.a` there is no spec module reference and records nothing, while the top-level chain is rooted at the spec module import — `occurrences --file src/y.ts`, answering on the failing workspace (11.2), lists exactly one record beside the finding, the top-level marker targeting `specs/A.mdx#a.b`, its source `src/y.ts` — failing a product that judges cross-scope collisions between spec module imports alone, which reports nothing, and one rooting the inner chain at the outer import, which lists a second record, targeting `specs/A.mdx#a`. (f) The file boundary — an import's scope is at widest its own file's (4.5: never a block's, as TypeScript at release 5.9.3 declares them, its binder placing a script's block-nested import in that file's own symbol table; 6.5: a nested import's binding visible only within the import's own scope) — `src/p.ts` = `{ import A from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, and `src/q.ts` = `A.a.b`, U+000A, each a script to TypeScript, no top-level import or export making it a module, so that TypeScript's checker merges their file-level declarations into one global scope and resolves `q.ts`'s `A` to `p.ts`'s import: under 4.5 that import binds in `p.ts`'s scope alone, so `q.ts`'s chain is rooted at nothing — `build` and `check` exit 0, `query edges` lists no edge from `src/q.ts`, and `occurrences --file src/q.ts` lists no record — failing a product that roots chains through a whole-program checker, which records the edge. (b)'s if-block and bare-block files, scripts alike and sharing one workspace, each root their chain at their own import by the same reading. (g) The callee side of the visibility boundary (4.5: an import declaration declares its bindings, its `text` binding included, in its own scope, and a `text` binding is sanctioned only as the callee of a spec module's `text` call; 6.5: a nested import's binding visible only within the import's own scope) — on a workspace of its own, `src/c.ts` = `import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `export function g() { import { text } from "../specs/A.xspec" }`, U+000A, `text(SPEC.a)`, U+000A: the nested `text` binding is `g`'s, so no `text` binding is visible at the top-level call, whose callee resolves to no declaration, as TypeScript 5.9.3 resolves it — the call is no spec module's `text` call, and the node `SPEC.a` is passed to another function (4.5): `build` and `check` report exactly one finding, 14.18 located at `SPEC.a`, [105, 111) (T14-11), exit 1 — no 14.7, 14.8, or 14.11 — and the call records no `embeds` edge and no occurrence (5.7), `occurrences --file src/c.ts`, answering on the failing workspace (11.2), listing no record beside the finding; and, on a workspace of its own, the same file with `text("x")` in the call's place reports no finding, exit 0 — never 14.8, the string argument being no spec module `text` call's (4.3) — `query edges` listing no edge from `src/c.ts` and `occurrences --file src/c.ts` no record. A product resolving a callee through every `text` binding in the file, nested ones included, reads each call as A's `text` call — recording the first's `embeds` edge and occurrence and reporting nothing, and reporting 14.8 for the second — failing both stagings; T4-6(b) is the visible side's control, and T6.5-24(e) the move-side counterpart. (h) Scopes nested within the import's own (2.4, 4.5: which binding roots a spelling is the language's scoping question, TypeScript resolving an identifier through each enclosing scope outward; 6.5: a nested import's binding visible within the import's own scope, the scopes nested in it included) — on a workspace of its own, `src/c.ts` = `export function g() {`, U+000A, `  import A, { text } from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `  const h = () => { A.a.b }`, U+000A, `  function k() { text(A.a) }`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A: the marker in the arrow function `h` and the call in the function `k`, each standing in a scope nested within `g`'s where no declaration shadows `A` or `text`, are rooted at `g`'s import, as TypeScript 5.9.3 resolves them — `build` and `check` exit 0, `query edges` reports one `references` edge from `src/c.ts#g.h` (4.6) to `specs/A.mdx#a.b` and one `embeds` edge from `src/c.ts#g.k` to `specs/A.mdx#a`, and `occurrences --file src/c.ts` exactly two records, a marker occurrence spanning `A.a.b`, [87, 92), its source `src/c.ts#g.h`, and a call occurrence spanning `text(A.a)`, [112, 121), its source `src/c.ts#g.k` — failing a product that matches a nested import only against the innermost function, class static block, or namespace or module declaration enclosing the spelling, never walking outward, which finds both spellings rooted at nothing and records neither edge; the move-side counterpart is T6.5-24(g).
* **T4.5-11 Collisions in the import's own scope.** A spec module import's binding collides with another import, or — the import's binding being value-level (4) — with a value-level non-import declaration, of the import's own scope (2.4, 4.5, 14.15): for a nested import, that of the innermost function, class static block, or namespace or module declaration enclosing it (T4.5-10), a function's scope holding its parameters too (2.4), while a declaration of an inner scope — a block's — shadows the binding instead, and one of an enclosing scope is shadowed by it, colliding with nothing (4.5: shadowing is an inner scope's; (h)); and two imports binding one identifier are the 14.15 collision whatever scope each declares it in, each still rooting its own scope's chains and binding its own scope's callees where no other declaration of that scope binds the identifier (T4.5-10(d); the callee side, (i)). Each staging a workspace of its own, its code source `src/c.ts` as given, `specs/A.mdx` and `specs/B.mdx` each holding `a` and `b` so that either module's binding alone would resolve every chain, each file text TypeScript 5.9.3 accepts both as module code and as script code (S-9) and none compiled under H-2's channel (T4.5-10); outcomes observed through `build` and `check`, through `occurrences --file src/c.ts` on a failing workspace (11.2), where `query edges` is gated (13.3), and through `query edges` on a valid one, each condition-15 finding locating every colliding declaration by 14's rules (T14-11): (a) a parameter — `src/c.ts` = `import { text } from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `export function g(SPEC: number) {`, U+000A, `  import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `  SPEC.a`, U+000A, `  text(SPEC.b)`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A: `build` and `check` report 14.15 locating the nested import and the parameter `SPEC: number` by its own characters, and 14.7 for the marker and for the call — its callee the top-level `text`, its argument rooted at the colliding identifier — exit 1, no edge and no occurrence for either spelling; likewise a constructor parameter property — `src/c.ts` = `export class C {`, U+000A, `  constructor(@dec private readonly SPEC: number) {`, U+000A, `    import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `    SPEC.a`, U+000A, `  }`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A — 14.15 locating the import and the parameter from its `@` (14: a parameter by its own characters, its decorators and modifiers included), and 14.7 for `SPEC.a`; likewise a parameter spelled beyond a name and a type annotation, one staging each — an initializer, a rest parameter, and a binding pattern (the twin of T4.5-8's declarator binding-pattern arm): `src/c.ts` = `export function g(SPEC = 1) {`, `export function g(...SPEC: number[]) {`, or `export function g({ SPEC }) {`, then U+000A, `  import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `  SPEC.a`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A — 14.15 locating the import and the parameter by its own characters, initializer, `...`, and pattern included — `SPEC = 1`, `...SPEC: number[]`, and `{ SPEC }`, each whole — and 14.7 for `SPEC.a`, a product locating a parameter from its name through its type annotation, or a pattern's element alone, failing these; and the contrast — the same parameter beside a top-level import shadows it instead (2.4: a declaration of an inner scope): `src/c.ts` = `import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `export function g(SPEC: number) {`, U+000A, `  SPEC.a`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A, `SPEC.b`, U+000A — no finding, exit 0, `SPEC.a` recording no edge (T4.5-4's outcome) and `SPEC.b` its `references` edge from `src/c.ts` to `specs/A.mdx#b` — a product ignoring parameters failing every staging before the contrast, and one reading every same-named parameter as a collision failing the contrast; a `var` in an inner block, which the language declares in the enclosing function's scope, never the block's, collides alike — `src/c.ts` = `export function g() {`, U+000A, `  {`, U+000A, `    var SPEC = 1`, U+000A, `  }`, U+000A, `  import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `  SPEC.a`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A, TypeScript 5.9.3's binder declaring the `var` and the import in `g`'s one scope: 14.15 locating the import and the declarator `SPEC = 1`, and 14.7 for `SPEC.a`, exit 1, no edge and no occurrence — failing a product that scopes every declaration by its textual block, which finds the `var` shadowing within its block alone and `SPEC.a` rooted at the import, recording its edge and reporting nothing; and the contrast — the declaration in an inner block holding the import shadows instead, the import's binding belonging to the function and the declaration to the block: `src/c.ts` = `export function g() {`, U+000A, `  {`, U+000A, `    const SPEC = 1`, U+000A, `    import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `    SPEC.a`, U+000A, `  }`, U+000A, `  SPEC.b`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A — no finding, exit 0, the chain inside the block rooted at the local, recording no edge, and the chain after the block rooted at the import, recording its `references` edge from `src/c.ts#g` to `specs/A.mdx#b` — failing a product that judges a collision by the block the declarations stand in, which reports 14.15; and a catch clause's parameter, which belongs to the catch clause's scope, inner to the function's, shadows likewise — `src/c.ts` = `export function g() {`, U+000A, `  import SPEC from "../specs/A.xspec"`, U+000A, `  try {} catch (SPEC) {`, U+000A, `    SPEC.a`, U+000A, `  }`, U+000A, `  SPEC.b`, U+000A, `}`, U+000A: no finding, exit 0, `SPEC.a` rooted at the catch parameter, recording no edge, and `SPEC.b` at the import, recording its `references` edge from `src/c.ts#g` to `specs/A.mdx#b` — failing a product that counts a catch parameter among the function's own parameters or variables, which reports 14.15 and 14.7;
